A 2-complex containing Sobolev spaces of matrix fields
Jay Gopalakrishnan, Kaibo Hu, Joachim Schöberl

Abstract
. Using a generalization of complexes, called 2-complexes, this paper defines and analyzes new Sobolev spaces of matrix fields and their interrelationships within a commuting diagram. These spaces have very weak second-order derivatives. An example is the space of matrix fields of square-integrable components whose row-wise divergence followed by yet another divergence operation yield a function in a standard negative-order Sobolev space. Similar spaces where the double divergence is replaced by a curl composed with divergence, or a double curl operator (the incompatibility operator), are also studied. Stable decompositions of such spaces in terms of more regular component functions (which are continuous in natural norms) are established. Appropriately ordering such Sobolev spaces with and without boundary conditions (in a weak sense), we discover duality relationships between them. Motivation to study such Sobolev spaces, from a finite element perspective and implications for weak well-posed variational formulations are pointed out. Keywords: Sobolev spaces, regular decomposition, 2-complex, Hilbert complexes. MSC2020: 58J10, 58A12, 35J58, 35B65.



1.
Introduction

Substantial improvements in numerical techniques for solving partial
differential equations (PDEs) to address current scientific challenges
have come from connections to and preservation of the differential and
algebraic structures inherent in the PDEs. Ample examples are offered
by the history of finite element techniques. The earliest finite
elements
[
17
]
, Lagrange elements, consisted of
scalar-valued
functions. Developments in
vector-valued
finite
elements followed, starting with elements
[
39
]
of
continuous normal (
n
) components. These “
n
-continuous” elements
were supplemented with “
t
-continuous” vector-valued
Nédélec elements with continuous tangential (
t
)
components
[
32
]
. Further families of vector-valued elements
were unearthed continuing this line of work. Although these elements
were developed separately, today we understand them together as
fitting into a cochain subcomplex of a de Rham complex of Sobolev
spaces, thanks to intensive research into finite element exterior
calculus
(FEEC)
[
4
,
6
,
25
]
.
It is now clear how to generalize from scalar and vector fields to
tensor fields, as long as the tensors have the algebraic structure of

k
-forms in the de Rham complex, i.e., higher order
alternating
tensor-valued
finite elements in any dimension naturally fit into
FEEC.

This paper, while building on these developments, is motivated by
other types of tensors. Problems in continuum mechanics, differential
geometry and general relativity call for a study of tensors with other
types of symmetries. Indeed, even restricting to second-order tensors,
the need for study is evident from the increasing current interest in
matrix-valued finite element functions. The earliest of these are the
“
nn
-continuous” symmetric matrix fields (i.e., symmetric
matrix-valued functions
\sigma
with continuous
(\sigma n)\cdot n
)
of the HHJ (Hellan-Herrmann-Johnson) elements
[
15
]
, now
enjoying a revival
[
7
,
40
,
37
,
38
]
in the TDNNS method
and elsewhere. A seemingly disjoint (but potentially connected)
recent development is the “
nt
-continuous” trace-free matrix finite
element developed
[
20
,
21
,
19
]
for viscous fluid stresses in the context of the
MCS (Mass-Conserving Stress-yielding) method. To add to this picture,
Regge
elements
[
14
,
29
,
22
,
23
]

with “
tt
-continuous” symmetric matrix-valued elements are finding
more and more uses. How does one connect these disparate developments
of
nn
,
nt
, and
tt
-continuous matrix finite elements? The prior
synthesis (mentioned in the previous paragraph) involved spaces of the
de Rham complex, all connected by fundamental first-order differential
operators (grad, curl, and divergence, in three dimensions). In
contrast, what seems to be natural for the matrix finite elements are
other
second-order
differential operators.

The goal of this work is to take a step toward understanding what

Sobolev spaces
and their arrangements might reveal a unified structure
where such second-order differential operators and matrix fields arise
naturally. Although motivated by finite elements, this work does not
contain finite elements. The scope is limited to a study of
infinite-dimensional Sobolev spaces of matrix fields, their
interrelationships, and connections to standard Sobolev spaces. We
focus on spaces of scalar, vector, and matrix valued functions on

three-dimensional
(3D) domains
\varOmega
. Study of higher
order tensor fields on higher dimensional domains is certainly
interesting, but requires more algebraic machinery (such as group
representations and Young tableaux) to work with tensor symmetries.

In 3D however, the relevant symmetries can be captured by the familiar
symmetrization and deviatoric operations,

\operatorname{sym}\tau=\frac{1}{2}(\tau+\tau^{\T}),\qquad\operatorname{dev}\tau=\tau-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{tr}(\tau){\mathbbm{i}},\qquad\tau\in\mathbb{{M}},

(1)

where
\mathbb{{M}}=\mathbb{{R}}^{3\times 3}
denotes the vector space of

3\times 3
real (
\mathbb{{R}}
) matrices,
\operatorname{tr}(\tau)
denotes the trace of a matrix

\tau\in\mathbb{{M}}
, and
{\mathbbm{i}}
denotes the
3\times 3
identity matrix.
Here and throughout,
\tau^{\T}
, also written as
\T\tau
, denotes
the (pointwise) transpose of a matrix field
\tau
. The operations
in (
1
) generate subspaces of symmetric matrices and
trace-free matrices which we denote by

\mathbb{{S}}=\operatorname{sym}\mathbb{{M}},\qquad\mathbb{{T}}=\operatorname{dev}\mathbb{{M}}.

Let
\mathbb{{V}}=\mathbb{{R}}^{3}
. We are interested in structures
connecting Sobolev spaces of functions with values in
\mathbb{{R}}
,

\mathbb{{V}}
,
\mathbb{{S}}
and
\mathbb{{T}}
of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}

(2)

Such diagrams where
\mathbb{{R}}
,
\mathbb{{V}}
,
\mathbb{{S}}
and
\mathbb{{T}}
are replaced
by appropriate Sobolev spaces of functions taking values in them, are
studied here. The first such diagram is introduced below
in (
25
), which contain first-order derivative operators as
well as key algebraic operations
\T
,
\operatorname{sym},
and
\operatorname{dev}
. Certain
combinations of these operations result in basic second-order
derivative operators marked in diagram (
37
).

The tensors along the four edges of (
2
) follow the

\mathbb{R}
-
{\mathbb{V}}
-
{\mathbb{V}}
-
\mathbb{R}
pattern of the well-known 3D de Rham complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}

(3)

of infinitely smooth
(C^{\infty})
scalar and vector fields on
\varOmega
.
Recall that a “complex” is a sequence of linear spaces
X_{i}
and
linear maps
A_{i}:X_{i}\to X_{i+1}
, traditionally expressed by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots\quad X_{k-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k+2}\quad\cdots,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k-2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k+1}}

(4)

satisfying
A_{i+1}\circ A_{i}=0
for all
i
.
In
[
33
]
, “
\ell
-complexes” arose, which are
sequences (
4
) with the property

A_{i+\ell}\circ\cdots\circ A_{i+1}\circ A_{i}=0
for all
i
and
some fixed integer
\ell
(so, e.g., a 1-complex is a complex in the
usual sense). As we shall see, diagrams of the form (
2
)
that we study here have a 2-complex structure (which explains the
title of this paper). Definition
1.1
below formalizes
the 2-complex notion in the context of such diagrams.

Other examples of complexes, beyond the de Rham
complex (
3
), include the well-known elasticity
complex
[
2
,
8
,
5
,
35
,
28
]
,
also named after
Calabi or Kröner,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{V}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{curl}\T\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}

(5)

the hessian complex
[
2
,
26
]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{V}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}

(6)

and the
\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}
complex
[
2
,
34
]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}}

(7)

These complexes can be systematically derived from the de Rham
complex (
3
) using the Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand
(BGG) construction, originally developed in algebraic and geometric
contexts
[
13
,
9
]
and more
recently adapted to certain Sobolev spaces
[
2
,
12
]
.
We shall see that analogous complexes,
with other “
H^{-1}
based” Sobolev spaces,
defined shortly in
(
23
)–(
24
), also arise
naturally from the 2-complexes and the diagrams of the
type (
2
) that we study here.

On the theme of
weakly regular
H^{-1}
based Sobolev spaces,

which is pervasive in this paper,
some motivating examples shed more light. Let

(u,v):=\int_{\varOmega}uv\;dx

(8)

for scalar fields
u,v
, and in addition, for vector or matrix fields

u
and
v
, we continue to use the same notation
(u,v)
to denote
the Lebesgue integral (when it exists) over
\varOmega
of the dot product

u\cdot v
, or the Frobenius product
u:v
, respectively, of
u
and

v
. All function spaces on
\varOmega
are defined precisely in
Subsection
1.1
, but for expediency, we use
the standard space
L_{2}
and the space
H^{-1}
with weaker topology
in the quick discussion of two examples below, both showing the role
of weak regularity, and each illuminating the role of one of two
algebraic operations
\operatorname{sym}
and
\operatorname{dev}
.

Example 1
.

The stress tensor
\sigma
in linear elasticity is a matrix field
which must satisfy
\sigma=\operatorname{sym}(\sigma)
due to conservation of
angular momentum. Well-posed formulations for the
Hellinger-Reissner principle in linear elasticity seek a symmetric
matrix field (the stress tensor)
\sigma:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{S}}
in some
Sobolev space
\Sigma
and a vector field
u:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{V}}

(displacement) in some Sobolev space
V
satisfying

\displaystyle(A\sigma,\tau)+(u,\operatorname{div}\tau)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ for all }\tau\in\Sigma,

(9)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{div}\sigma,v)

\displaystyle=(f,v)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }v\in V,

where
A
,
f
, and
\operatorname{div}
denotes the compliance tensor, the load
vector field, and row-wise divergence of a matrix field,
respectively. A “regular choice” is
V=L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}
and

\Sigma=\{\tau\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}:\operatorname{div}\tau\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}\}.

(10)

Construction of finite elements for this
\Sigma
is difficult and
had remained an open problem for decades, as noted
in
[
3
]
. (If the symmetry condition on
\sigma
were
absent, then three copies of the
n
-continuous finite
elements would have been sufficient.)
An alternative choice of “weak regularity” is

\Sigma=\{\tau\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}:\operatorname{div}\tau\in V^{*}\}

(11)

where
V^{*}
is weaker than
L_{2}
integrals of
the form
(\operatorname{div}\tau,v)
in (
9
) are relaxed to a duality pairing

(\operatorname{div}\tau)(v)
in
V
. The TDNNS
method
[
37
,
38
]
with
nn
-continuous stresses
can be seen as a
discretization of such a formulation with
V={\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})
, a space
defined shortly in (
15
). Theorem
5.4
shows
that the condition
\operatorname{div}\tau\in V^{*}
in (
11
) is equivalent to

\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}\tau\in H^{-1}
. This motivates us to study
Sobolev spaces with this weak regularity condition, namely the
spaces
H_{\mathrm{dd}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{dd}}
defined in (
73c
) and
(
74b
), respectively.

Example 2
.

Viscous stresses in Stokes flow with fluid velocity

u:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{V}}
can be extracted from the symmetric part of

\sigma=2\nu\operatorname{grad}u
, where
\nu
is the kinematic viscosity. The
incompressibility constraint
\operatorname{div}u=0
, a well known source of
challenges in numerical simulation
[
27
]
, now
emerges as an algebraic constraint:
\sigma=\operatorname{dev}\sigma
. The
definition of
\sigma
and flow equations suggest that we should
find
\sigma
in a space
\Sigma
of trace-free matrix fields,
u

in some space
V
of vector fields,
and the pressure
p
in some space
Q
of scalar
fields such that

\displaystyle(\nu^{-1}\sigma,\tau)+(u,\operatorname{div}\tau)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{for all }\tau\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle(\operatorname{div}\sigma,v)+(\operatorname{div}v,p)

\displaystyle=-(f,v)

\displaystyle\text{for all }v\in V,

\displaystyle(\operatorname{div}u,q)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{for all }p\in Q.

for some given source field
f
. The MCS
method
[
20
,
21
,
19
]
sets
V={\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{div})
(a
space defined shortly in (
15
)) and
Q=\operatorname{div}V
. Then,
instead of a “regular choice”

\Sigma=\{\tau\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{T}}:\operatorname{div}\sigma\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}\}
that would make the integrals like

(\operatorname{div}\sigma,v)
well defined, the MCS formulation proposes a
choice of “weak regularity,” namely

\Sigma=\{\tau\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{T}}:\operatorname{div}\sigma\in V^{*}\}

(12)

for which simple
nt
-continuous finite elements work, after relaxing

(\operatorname{div}\sigma,v)
to a duality pairing
(\operatorname{div}\sigma)(v)
in
V
.
Theorem
5.4
shows
that the condition
\operatorname{div}\tau\in V^{*}
in (
12
) is equivalent to

\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\tau\in H^{-1}
. This motivates us to study
Sobolev spaces with this weak regularity condition, namely the
spaces
H_{\mathrm{cd}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{cd}}
defined in (
73b
) and
(
74c
), respectively.

1.1.
Preliminaries and spaces

Let
\varOmega
be a bounded open connected subset of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{{R}}^{3}

with Lipschitz boundary. Let
L_{2}
denote the space of
square-integrable
\mathbb{{R}}
-valued functions on
\varOmega
, or equivalently
the space of square-integrable
\mathbb{{R}}
-valued functions on
\mathbb{{R}}^{3}

supported on
\bar{\varOmega}
.
Let
\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)
denote the
Schwartz space of smooth test functions on
\varOmega
that are compactly
supported in
\varOmega
. The dual of any topological space
X
is denoted
by
X^{*}
. The space of distributions on
\varOmega
is denoted by

\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)^{*}
. The space of vector fields on
\varOmega
with square-integrable
components is denoted by
L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}
and the notation is
similarly extended to
\mathbb{{T}}
and
\mathbb{{S}}
-valued fields on
\varOmega
as
well as to other spaces, e.g.,
\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)^{*}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}
denotes the space of
symmetric matrix-valued fields whose components are distributions on
\varOmega
.

We use the standard Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})

and
H^{s}(\varOmega)
for any
s\in\mathbb{{R}}
. (see e.g.,
[
1
,
30
]
). We omit the domain
\varOmega
from
the notation when no confusion can arise and simply write
H^{s}
for

H^{s}(\varOmega)
. For scalar functions
u:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{R}}
and vector
functions
v,q:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{V}}
, let

\begin{gathered}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}=\|u\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{grad}u\|_{L_{2}}^{2},\\
\|v\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}^{2}=\|v\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{curl}v\|_{L_{2}}^{2},\qquad\|q\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}^{2}=\|q\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}v\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.\end{gathered}

(13)

These are norms of well-known Hilbert spaces of functions on
\varOmega
,
namely

\displaystyle H(\operatorname{grad})

\displaystyle\equiv H^{1}=\{u\in L_{2}:\operatorname{grad}u\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}\},

(14a)

\displaystyle H(\operatorname{curl})

\displaystyle=\{v\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}:\operatorname{curl}v\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}\},

(14b)

\displaystyle H(\operatorname{div})

\displaystyle=\{q\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{{V}}:\operatorname{div}q\in L_{2}\}.

(14c)

Using the standard norms in (
13
), the closures

{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{grad})=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)}^{\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}}},\quad{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}},\quad{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{div})=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}},

(15)

give well-known zero-trace subspaces of the spaces
in (
14
).

Further spaces are defined using similar closures, but using the set of

\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)
-functions extended by zero to all
\mathbb{{R}}^{3}
and
closing the set using the
H^{s}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})
norm.
Set

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)}^{\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})}},\qquad s\in\mathbb{{R}},

(16)

and
\|u\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}}:=\|u\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})}.
This space is often
just denoted by
\widetilde{H}^{s}(\varOmega)
, and in our setting, is also the
same as another often-occurring space in the literature,

H^{s}_{\overline{\varOmega}}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})=\{u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3}):\;\operatorname{supp}u\subset\bar{\varOmega}\}
(see e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 3.29]
), i.e.,

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}=\{u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3}):\;\operatorname{supp}u\subset\bar{\varOmega}\}.

(17)

Since the
i
th
partial derivative
\partial_{i}
satisfies

\|\partial_{i}\varphi\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}}\leq\|\varphi\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s+1}}}}
for all

\varphi\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)
, and since
\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)
is dense in
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}

by definition (
16
), we conclude that for any real
s
,

\partial_{i}:\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s+1}}}\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}\quad\text{is continuous}.

(18)

It is well known
[
30
, Theorem 3.30]
that
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}
is also
identifiable with a standard dual space

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}=(H^{-s})^{*}

(19)

for any
s\in\mathbb{{R}}
.
The case
s=-1
is of particular interest here. The space
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}
, not
to be confused with
H^{-1}={\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{grad})^{*}
, satisfies,
per (
19
),

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=H(\operatorname{grad})^{*},

(20)

and furthermore, even if
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}
is not embedded in a space of distributions on

\varOmega
, it can be characterized using tempered distributions on

\mathbb{{R}}^{3}
supported on the closure of
\varOmega
, due to (
17
).
Therefore the norm of any
u
in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}
can be computed either using
the
H^{-1}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})
-norm of the extension of
u
by zero to all

\mathbb{{R}}^{3}
, or by duality using (
20
). Finally, we note that it is also well-known
[
30
, Theorem 3.33]
when
s>0
,
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}
is contained in

{\mathring{H}}^{s}:=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)}^{\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}(\varOmega)}}

and, moreover,
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}
and
{\mathring{H}}^{s}
are equal if
s>0
and

s-\frac{1}{2}
is not an integer, so e.g.,
{\mathring{H}}^{1}=\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{1}}
.

For a general
s\in\mathbb{{R}}
, we define the norms

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|v\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{curl}v\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}}^{2},

\displaystyle\|q\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{div})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|q\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}q\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}}^{2}.

and set

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})}},\qquad\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{div})=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{div})}}.

(21)

These spaces with
s=-1
feature in a central diagram introduced shortly.

Next we introduce key spaces of matrix-valued
fields, which are also needed for the diagram.
Note that when the standard differential operators
\operatorname{div}
and

\operatorname{curl}
are applied to matrix-valued fields, we do so row-wise. The
next definitions involve second-order differential operators on
matrix-valued functions
g:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{S}}
,
\tau:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{T}}
, and

\sigma:\varOmega\to\mathbb{{S}}
, such as the incompatibility operator

\operatorname{inc}g:=\operatorname{curl}\T\operatorname{curl}g.

(22)

Let

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|g\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{curl}g\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{inc}g\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}

(23a)

\displaystyle\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\tau\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}\tau\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T\tau\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\tau\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}

(23b)

\displaystyle\|\sigma\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\sigma\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}\sigma\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}\sigma\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2},

(23c)

and
\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}\scalebox{0.6}{$\top$}}}=\|\T\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{S}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}}},\quad\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{T}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}},\quad\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}=\overline{\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{S}}}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}}}.

(24)

The space
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}\scalebox{0.6}{$\top$}}=\{\tau^{\T}:\;\tau\in\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}\}
will also be
needed. Clearly, in view of (
16
), the spaces

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}},\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
and
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}
are subspaces of
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}
,

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}\otimes\mathbb{{T}}
, and
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}\otimes\mathbb{{S}}
, respectively.

Certain subspaces of
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}
,
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})
and
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{div})
, which we now define, occur often. Let

\mathcal{P}_{1}
denote the space of linear polynomials. Using the coordinate
vector
x
in
\mathbb{{R}}^{3}
, define

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}=\{a+bx:a\in\mathbb{{V}},b\in\mathbb{{R}}\},\qquad\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}=\{a+d\times x:a,d\in\mathbb{{V}}\}.

Let

\displaystyle L_{2,\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\{u\in L_{2}:\;(u,1)=0\},\quad\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathbb{{R}}}=\{u\in\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}:\;u(1)=0\},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}(\operatorname{curl})

\displaystyle=\{v\in\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl}):v(r)=0\text{ for all }r\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}\},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}(\operatorname{div})

\displaystyle=\{q\in\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{div}):q(r)=0\text{ for all }r\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}\},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{w\in\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}:\;w(p)=0\text{ for all }p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\}.

Here and throughout, the action of a distribution
w
on a function

p
in
\mathcal{{D}}(\mathbb{{R}}^{3})
is denoted by
w(p)
. In the above subspaces
of distributions, note that only the value of
p|_{\varOmega}
on
\varOmega
is needed
to evaluate the action
w(p)
since
w
is supported on
\bar{\varOmega}
.
Note also that
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}(\operatorname{curl})
and
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}(\operatorname{div})

are closed subspaces of
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})
and
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{div})
.

1.2.
A diagram connecting the Sobolev spaces

Using the above-defined notation, we can now precisely
introduce one of the objects of study in this paper. It is
the following diagram
connecting the above-defined Sobolev spaces of scalar-, vector-, and
matrix-valued distributions on
\mathbb{{R}}^{3}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{grad})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{div})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{2,\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{div})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}\scalebox{0.6}{$\top$}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{2,\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{def}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{2}\T\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{def}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\T\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}\T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{grad}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{div}}

(25)

Here
\operatorname{def}u=\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{grad}u
for vector fields
u
denotes the deformation operator, where
\operatorname{grad}u
is
the matrix field whose
(i,j)
th component is
\partial u_{i}/\partial x_{j}
.
Note that information in the diagram (
25
) is repeated
across the diagonal, i.e., the diagram is symmetric about the
diagonal.
The properties collected in the next section show that each of the
indicated operators is linear and continuous in the norms of
the indicated domain and codomain, and that each component cell in the
diagram commutes. A different but similar diagram starting with analogous
spaces without boundary conditions

H(\operatorname{grad}),H(\operatorname{curl}),
and
H(\operatorname{div})
, is found later in Section
5
.

In the commutative diagram (
25
), the “objects” (or
“vertices”) are the spaces. The “morphisms” (or “arrows”) are
the indicated first-order differential operators. Compositions of
morphisms are referred to as “paths”. Clearly, paths in (
25
)
always go right or down from an object. The following definition of a
“2-complex” is motivated by
[
33
]
.

Definition 1.1
.

A path is a
complex
if the composition of two successive
morphisms in it vanish. We say that a path is a
2-complex
if
the composition of three successive morphisms in it vanish.

We show (in the next section, in Theorem
2.4
) that all
paths in the diagram (
25
) are 2-complexes. The analogous
diagram for spaces without boundary conditions also shares the same
property, as we shall see in Section
5
.

Before concluding this introduction, a few remarks on comparison with the BGG approach are in order. The BGG construction of
[
2
,
12
]
produces
analogues of (
5
), (
6
)
and (
7
) with Sobolev spaces

H^{q}\otimes\mathbb{W}
or

H(D,\mathbb{W}):=\{\sigma\in L_{2}\otimes\mathbb{W}:D\sigma\in L_{2}\otimes\tilde{\mathbb{W}}\},
for appropriate

\mathbb{{W}},\tilde{\mathbb{{W}}}\in\{\mathbb{{S}},\mathbb{{T}}\}
and operators
D
from
the above complexes. The hessian, elasticity, and div-div complexes
were also studied individually in other
works
[
36
,
18
,
34
]
. It
should not be surprising that some individual results in this paper
may be alternately derived using the prior approaches, e.g., the
commutativity identities (
26
) are extensively
used in BGG works, and the regular decomposition for two of the
“slightly more regular spaces”
in Section
4
,

\,\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}
and
\,\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{\!\mathcal{H}}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}
, can be approached using the
technique of
[
2
, Theorem 3]
with minor changes.
However, such individual results do not fully address the objectives of this
paper. For instance, the spaces defined in (
23
)
do not emerge from
[
2
,
12
]
as canonical spaces
with a unified definition; rather, they exhibit a cohesive pattern
only through the perspective of the 2-complexes
in (
25
). Consequently, the analytical results for these spaces,
such as regular decompositions, differ significantly from those
in
[
2
,
12
]
. Moreover, the 2-complex
in (
25
) unifies several key spaces, including the Hessian,
elasticity, and
\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}
complexes, potentially inspiring novel
constructions across diverse applications. This unification can be
reminiscent of the BGG diagram
[
2
,
12
]
. However,
a critical distinction is that BGG diagrams involve full matrix spaces
requiring subsequent symmetry reduction, whereas (
25
) directly
incorporates spaces of tensors with the symmetrizations.

1.3.
Outline

The next section (Section
2
) begins by gathering a
number of identities from which the commutativity properties in the diagram (
25
) become
evident. We prove the 2-complex property of (
25
), show how the
elasticity complex, the hessian complex and the div-div complex
emerges from the diagram. In Section
3
, we prove
that the newly introduced
H^{-1}
based Sobolev spaces of weak regularity admit
decompositions with smoother component functions that vary
continuously with the decomposed function
(Theorems
3.4
,
3.7
, and

3.10
). We construct right inverses (in
Theorem
3.21
) of the operators in (
25
) as well
as of second-order differential operators that emerge from the
diagram, from which it follows that the ranges of the differential
operators considered are closed. This can be used to prove exactness
of derived complexes. Slightly smoother versions of the matrix-valued
Sobolev spaces are then considered in
Section
4
and shorter regular
decompositions for them are proved. Finally, in
Section
5
, we mention extensions to the case of
analogous spaces without boundary conditions. The main result of that
section is Theorem
5.4
which shows how the diagrams of spaces with
and without boundary conditions are in correspondence through duality.

2.
Continuity, commutativity, and 2-complex properties

In this section, we show that the diagram (
25
) is a commuting
diagram and has the 2-complex property.

In addition to
\operatorname{sym},\operatorname{dev},\operatorname{tr}
and
\operatorname{skw}\tau=\tau-\operatorname{sym}\tau
,
we use the algebraic operation
S:\mathbb{{M}}\to\mathbb{{M}}
defined by

S\tau=\tau^{\T}-\operatorname{tr}(\tau){\mathbbm{i}},

whose inverse can be easily computed to be

S^{-1}\tau=\tau^{\T}-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(\tau){\mathbbm{i}}.

We often use the summation convention and the alternating symbol

\varepsilon^{ijk}\equiv\varepsilon_{ijk}
whose value equals
+1,-1,
or 0
according to whether
ijk
is a even, odd or not a permutation of 1,
2, 3. Using Cartesian unit vectors
e_{i}\equiv e^{i}
and the summation
convention, we write a vector
v
as
v=v_{i}e^{i}
. Using
\varepsilon
,
one can express an isomorphism between skew-symmetric matrices in

\mathbb{{K}}=\operatorname{skw}\mathbb{{M}}
and their axial vectors in
\mathbb{{V}}
, given by

\operatorname{mskw}:\mathbb{{V}}\to\mathbb{{K}}
,

\operatorname{mskw}(v^{i}e_{i})=-\varepsilon^{ijk}v_{k}e_{i}\otimes e_{j}
. Let

\operatorname{vskw}:\mathbb{{M}}\to\mathbb{{V}}
be defined by

\operatorname{vskw}=\operatorname{mskw}^{-1}\circ\operatorname{skw}.
For distributional fields
w
,
vector fields
v
and matrix fields
\tau
on three-dimensional
domains, it is easy to see that the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\operatorname{mskw}v=-\operatorname{curl}v,

(26a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{mskw}\operatorname{grad}w=-\operatorname{curl}(w{\mathbbm{i}}),

(26b)

\displaystyle\operatorname{mskw}\operatorname{curl}v=2\operatorname{skw}\operatorname{grad}v,

(26c)

\displaystyle 2\operatorname{skw}\operatorname{curl}\tau=\operatorname{mskw}\operatorname{div}S\tau,

(26d)

\displaystyle S\operatorname{grad}v=-\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{mskw}v,

(26e)

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{curl}\tau=-2\operatorname{div}\operatorname{vskw}\tau,

(26f)

We start with two simple lemmas. Lemma
2.1

contains identities involving second-order partial differential
operators and Lemma
2.2
gives
density of the following smooth spaces with moment conditions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathbb{{R}}}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega):(\varphi,1)=0\},

(27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}:(\varphi,r)=0\text{ for all }r\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}:(\varphi,r)=0\text{ for all }r\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega):(\varphi,p)=0\text{ for all }p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}\}.

For any two norms
\|\cdot\|_{1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
,
we write

\|a\|_{1}\lesssim\|b\|_{2}

to indicate that there is some constant
C>0
independent of
a
and

b
such that the inequality
\|a\|_{1}\leq C\|b\|_{2}
holds.

Lemma 2.1
.

The identities

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\T\operatorname{curl}\tau=\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\T\tau,

(28)

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}\T\operatorname{grad}u=\T\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u=\T\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u,

(29)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T\tau=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\tau,

(30)

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{def}u=\frac{1}{2}\T\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u=\frac{1}{2}\T\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u,

(31)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{div}\T\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}u=\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{div}u

(32)

hold for any vector-valued distribution
u
and matrix-valued
distribution
\tau
.

Proof.

To prove (
28
), we express row-wise curl using
\varepsilon^{ijk}
, the
summation convention, and standard Cartesian unit vectors
e_{i}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\T\operatorname{curl}\tau

\displaystyle=e_{i}\partial_{j}[\T\operatorname{curl}\tau]^{ij}=e_{i}\partial_{j}[\operatorname{curl}\tau]^{ji}=e_{i}\partial_{j}\varepsilon^{ikl}\partial_{k}\tau_{jl}=e_{i}\varepsilon^{ikl}\partial_{k}\partial_{j}[\T\tau]_{lj}

\displaystyle=e_{i}\varepsilon^{ikl}\partial_{k}[\operatorname{div}\T\tau]_{l}=\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\T\tau.

The first equality in (
29
) is proved similarly. For the
second equality in (
29
), it suffices to note that the

(i,j)
th component of the matrix field
\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u
equals

\partial_{i}\varepsilon^{jkl}\partial_{k}u_{l}
, so its trace, obtained with
i=j
in
this expression, vanishes.

Identity (
30
) follows using
\operatorname{div}\circ\operatorname{curl}=0

and (
28
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T\tau

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\frac{1}{2}\big(\operatorname{curl}\T\tau+\T\operatorname{curl}\T\tau\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{div}\T\operatorname{curl}\T\tau=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\T\T\tau.

Equation (
31
) follows from (
29
) and

\operatorname{curl}\circ\operatorname{grad}=0
in an analogous fashion.
The proof of (
32
) using analogous techniques is also elementary.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

The spaces in (
27
), namely

\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathbb{{R}}}
,

\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}
,

\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}
, and

\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
, are dense in

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathbb{{R}}}

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}(\operatorname{curl})
,

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}(\operatorname{div})
, and

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
, respectively, for any
s\in\mathbb{{R}}
.

Proof.

The proofs of all the four stated density results are similar. We
only detail the second. Fix a nontrivial scalar function

b(x)\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)
satisfying
b(x)\geq 0
. Let
\rho_{i}
be a
basis of the four-dimensional space
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T},
normalized so that

(b\rho_{i},\rho_{j})=\delta_{ij}

(33)

where
\delta_{ij}
denotes the Kronecker delta symbol.
Let
v\in\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}(\operatorname{curl})
.
In view of (
21
), we can find a
sequence
\varphi_{n}\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\varphi_{n}-v\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})}=0.

(34)

Let

\psi_{n}(x)=\varphi_{n}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\varphi_{n},\rho_{i})\;b(x)\,\rho_{i}(x).

(35)

Then
\psi_{n}
is in
\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}
and

(\psi_{n},\rho_{j})=0
due to (
33
), i.e.,

\psi_{n}\in\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}
.
Moreover,
\psi_{n}
converges to
v
in
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}(\operatorname{curl})
as we now show:
indeed, since
v(\rho_{i})=0
,

(\varphi_{n},\rho_{i})=(\varphi_{n}-v)(\rho_{i})\leq\|\varphi_{n}-v\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}}\|\rho_{i}\|_{H^{-s}}

by (
19
). Hence
(
34
) implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}(\varphi_{n},\rho_{i})=0

(36)

for each
\rho_{i}
. Now it is evident from (
35
) that
\psi_{n}

converges to
v
in
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}
norm since
\varphi_{n}
does.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}(\psi_{n}-v)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{curl}(\varphi_{n}-v)-\sum_{i=1}^{4}(\varphi_{n},\rho_{i})\;\operatorname{curl}(b\,\rho_{i}),

where, on the right hand side, the first term converges to zero in

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}
by (
34
), and the second term converges to zero
by (
36
). Thus
\psi_{n}
and
\operatorname{curl}\psi_{n}
converges
in
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{s}}
to
v
and
\operatorname{curl}v
, respectively. Hence
\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}

is dense in
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{{{s}}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}(\operatorname{curl})
.
∎

Theorem 2.3
.

The diagram (
25
) commutes and every differential operator in
it maps continuously (with respect to the norms of the indicated
domains and codomains).

Proof.

Commutativity.

The commutativity of the diagram cells in (row, column)-positions

(2,3),(1,2),
and
(1,3)
follows respectively from
identities (
30
), (
31
), and (
32
) of
Lemma
2.1
. The commutativity at positions
across the diagonal also follow from these. At the remaining
positions, it is obvious.

Next, let us prove the stated continuity properties.
The continuity of the operators in the first row
and column is standard. For the remaining operators, we use
(
18
) and the following steps.
We begin the maps in the second row
of (
25
).

Continuity of
\operatorname{def}:{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}
.
For any

u\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}\subset{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})
, note that

g=\operatorname{def}u\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{S}}\subset\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}
satisfies

\operatorname{curl}g=\frac{1}{2}\T\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u=\frac{1}{2}\T\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u

due to (
31
) and (
29
). This implies that

\operatorname{inc}g:=\operatorname{curl}\T\operatorname{curl}g=0.

Hence, using (
18
),

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{def}u\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|g\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{curl}g\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{inc}g\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\operatorname{grad}u\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{curl}u\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}\lesssim\|u\|_{H(\operatorname{curl})}^{2},

which proves the continuity of the deformation operator by density.

Continuity of
\operatorname{curl}:\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
.
It suffices to
observe that
\tau=\operatorname{curl}g
, for any

g\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{S}}\subset\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}
, satisfies

\tau=\operatorname{dev}\tau\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{T}}\subset\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\tau

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\tau

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T\tau

\displaystyle=\operatorname{inc}g.

This shows that
\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}}
and the
continuity follows by density.

Continuity of
\operatorname{div}:\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}\to{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})
.
Let

\tau\in\mathcal{{\mathcal{D}}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{T}}\subset\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
. Then, for any

r=a+bx\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}
,
a\in\mathbb{{V}},b\in\mathbb{{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle(\operatorname{div}\tau,r)

\displaystyle=-(\tau,\operatorname{grad}r)=-(\tau,b{\mathbbm{i}})=0

since
\tau:{\mathbbm{i}}
vanishes for
\tau(x)\in\mathbb{{T}}
. Next, by the
definition of
\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
norm,

\displaystyle\|\operatorname{div}\tau\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\tau\|_{{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}}^{2}\leq\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}^{2}.

Since the left hand side equals
\|\operatorname{div}\tau\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}(\operatorname{curl})}^{2}
,
the continuity follows by density.

Continuity of
\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}:{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{div})\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}\scalebox{0.6}{$\top$}}
.

Let

\tau=\operatorname{dev}\operatorname{grad}q
for some

q\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{V}}\subset{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{div}).

Apply (
32
) to get

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\T\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\operatorname{grad}\operatorname{div}q,

which implies
\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\T\tau=0
. Also, since

\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\tau=\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\frac{1}{3}(\operatorname{div}q){\mathbbm{i}},

all terms in the norm
\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}\scalebox{0.6}{$\top$}}}
can be bounded by the

{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}
-norms of the first order of derivatives of
\operatorname{div}q
and

q
, so using (
18
),

\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}\scalebox{0.6}{$\top$}}}\lesssim\|q\|_{H(\operatorname{div})}
and the continuity
follows by density.

Continuity of
\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T:\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}
.
This is a
bounded operator since
\sigma=\operatorname{sym}\operatorname{curl}\T\tau
for any

\tau\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{T}}\subset\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
satisfies, due
to (
30
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\sigma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}\operatorname{div}\tau,

which in turn implies
\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}\sigma=0.
Thus

\|\sigma\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}}\lesssim\|\tau\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}}
.

Continuity of
\operatorname{div}:\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}\to{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})
.
First note that
for
any
\sigma\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\otimes\mathbb{{S}}\subset\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}

and
r=a+b\times x\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}
,
a,b\in\mathbb{{V}}
,

\displaystyle(\operatorname{div}\sigma,r)

\displaystyle=-(\sigma,\operatorname{grad}r)=0

because

\sigma:\operatorname{grad}r=\sigma_{ij}\partial_{j}[b\times x]_{i}=\varepsilon^{ijp}\sigma_{ij}b_{p}
vanishes due to the symmetry

\sigma_{ij}=\sigma_{ji}
. Thus

\operatorname{div}\sigma
is in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})
.
Moreover, by the definition of the

\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}
-norm

\|\operatorname{div}\sigma\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}+\|\operatorname{div}\operatorname{div}\sigma\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}}^{2}\leq\|\sigma\|_{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{dd}}}^{2}.

The left hand side exactly equals

\|\operatorname{div}\sigma\|_{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}}(\operatorname{div})}^{2}
so the continuity follows by density.

Continuity of
\operatorname{grad}:L_{2,\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})
.
Let

u\in\mathcal{{D}}(\varOmega)\cap L_{2,\mathbb{R}}=\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathbb{{R}}}
. By
Lemma
2.2

\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathbb{{R}}}
is dense in
L_{2,\mathbb{R}}
.
For any
r=a+bx\in\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}
,
a\in\mathbb{{V}},b\in\mathbb{{R}}
, integrating
by parts using the compact support of
u
,

\displaystyle(\operatorname{grad}u,r)=-(u,\operatorname{div}r)=-3(u,b)=0

since
u
has zero mean value on
\varOmega
. Hence
\operatorname{grad}u
is in

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})
, so by Lemma
2.2
,

\operatorname{grad}L_{2,\mathbb{R}}\subseteq{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})
.
The needed boundedness estimate is immediate
from (
18
).

Continuity of
\operatorname{curl}:{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})\to{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})
.

By Lemma
2.2
,
\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}
is dense in

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}}(\operatorname{curl})
. Let
v\in\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}}
. Then, given any

r=a+b\times x\in\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}
with some
a,b\in\mathbb{{V}}
,

(\operatorname{curl}v,r)=(v,\operatorname{curl}r)=2(v,b)=0

since
v
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{T}
. Hence
\operatorname{curl}v
is in

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})
. Combined with the obvious norm bound, the
continuity follows from density.

Continuity of
\operatorname{div}:{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})\to{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
.

Let
q\in\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}
. Then, for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
,

(\operatorname{div}q,p)=-(q,\operatorname{grad}p)=0

since
\operatorname{grad}p
is in
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}
and
q
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}
.
Therefore
\operatorname{div}q
is in
{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}}
. In view of the obvious
norm bound, the density of
\mathcal{{D}}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}
in

{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}^{-1}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{D}}}(\operatorname{div})
(given by Lemma
2.2
) finishes
the proof.
∎

Theorem 2.4
.

All paths in the diagram (
25
) are 2-complexes.

Proof.

Consider paths of the following form:

A
B
C

If the first path above is a 2-complex, then by the commutativity
properties of Theorem
2.3
, the second and
third are also 2-complexes. Proving that the first path is a
2-complex, i.e., showing that
C\circ B\circ A=0
for all such

A,B,C
in (
25
), is (tedious but) elementary using the identities of
(
26
) and Lemma
2.1
.
For example, with

A=\operatorname{grad}:{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{grad})\to{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl}),B=\operatorname{def}:{\mathring{H}}(\operatorname{curl})\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}},
and
C=\operatorname{curl}:\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cc}}\to\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathring{H}}$}}_{\mathrm{cd}}
,
we have

C\circ B\circ A=\operatorname{curl}\circ\frac{1}{2}(\operatorname{grad}+\T\operatorname{grad})\circ\operatorname{grad}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}\circ\T\operatorname{grad}\circ\operatorname{grad}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{curl}\circ\T\operatorname{grad}\circ\operatorname{grad}=\frac{1}{2}\T\operatorname{grad}\circ\operatorname{curl}\circ\operatorname{grad}=0
, where we have used the identity (
29
) of
Lemma
2.1
.
Similarly, it is elementary to see that
B\circ H\circ G=0

in paths taken from (
25
) of the form in the first diagram below,

G
H
B

so by commutativity, all paths of the three types shown above are
also 2-complexes. These types of paths exhaust all possibilities.
∎

.
